427	\section{Extraction Error Taxonomy}
428	\label{app:extraction}
429	\label{sec:extraction-taxonomy}
430	
431	Constraints must discriminate between candidate answers for MAX-SAT to produce a ranking different from majority vote. We measure discriminative power at two levels on Qwen2.5-14B across FOLIO and ProofWriter ($n = 804$). At the source-trace level, 39.2\% of total constraint weight originates from traces whose answer differs from the plurality, but only 12.1\% of individual constraints fall into this minority category. At the Z3 semantic level, 91.8\% of constraints [95\% CI: 91.2, 92.4] are technically discriminative, producing different satisfiability outcomes when evaluated against different candidate answers. Only 8.2\% are non-discriminative.
432	
433	The high discriminative rate does not translate into accuracy gains because discrimination mirrors the model's own answer distribution. Each trace produces constraints with its own variable names, so Z3 treats identically-intended propositions as unrelated symbols; among cross-answer constraint pairs, 56.4\% share zero Z3 variables. This variable isolation means constraints from different traces are trivially jointly satisfiable, and MAX-SAT retains nearly all of them regardless of source answer. Removing the 8.2\% non-discriminative constraints yields zero accuracy improvement. The discriminative majority does not provide independent signal; it reinforces the majority answer that SC would already select. This confirmation bias, not a lack of discrimination, explains why SICA degenerates to majority voting (Appendix~\ref{app:ablation}).
434	
435	
436	\section{Hyperparameter Sensitivity}
437	\label{app:sensitivity}
438	\label{sec:k-sensitivity}
439	\label{sec:t-sensitivity}
440	
441	\subsection{K Sensitivity}
442	
443	Figure~\ref{fig:k-sensitivity} shows SICA $\Delta$ as a function of the number of sampled traces $K$ for Mistral-7B on FOLIO ($n = 204$, seed 42). The relationship is non-monotonic: $K = 4$ yields $\Delta = -1.47$\,pp, $K = 8$ yields $\Delta = -1.96$\,pp, $K = 12$ reaches $\Delta = +3.43$\,pp, $K = 16$ drops to $\Delta = +1.47$\,pp, and $K = 20$ partially recovers to $\Delta = +1.96$\,pp. The recovery at $K = 20$ rules out a simple peaked pattern, but $K = 12$ remains the only setting with $\Delta > 2$\,pp ($p = 0.092$, marginal).
444	
445	The apparent $\Delta$ peak at $K = 12$ is confounded with SC baseline. SICA absolute accuracy plateaus at 59.31\% (121/204) for $K \geq 12$: identical at $K = 12$ and $K = 20$. The $\Delta$ variation across these settings is driven primarily by SC fluctuations rather than by SICA improvement. $K = 12$ produces a relatively low SC (55.88\%), placing it in the intermediate-SC region; $K = 8$ (SC $= 58.33\%$) and $K = 20$ (SC $= 57.35\%$) have higher baselines, compressing $\Delta$ even though SICA accuracy does not change for $K \geq 12$. Additional traces beyond $K = 12$ provide no benefit to SICA absolute performance, consistent with the constraint quality bottleneck. The $K = 12$ $\Delta$ captures only 24.1\% of the oracle ceiling ($+14.22$\,pp).
446	
447	\begin{figure}[h]
448	\centering
449	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{f3_k_sensitivity}
450	\caption{SICA $\Delta$ (pp) vs.\ number of traces $K$ for Mistral-7B on FOLIO ($n = 204$, seed 42, $T = 0.7$). Non-monotonic pattern: SICA accuracy plateaus at 59.31\% for $K \geq 12$; the apparent $\Delta$ peak at $K = 12$ partly reflects a relatively low SC baseline (55.88\%) at that point. Error bars: 95\% bootstrap CI.}
451	\label{fig:k-sensitivity}
452	\end{figure}
453	
454	\subsection{Temperature Sensitivity}
455	
456	Temperature sensitivity on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$, seed 42) reveals a sharp isolated peak rather than a smooth trend. Across four temperature settings, only $T = 0.7$ produces a positive effect: $T = 0.3$ yields $\Delta = -1.47$\,pp (SC $= 58.33\%$), $T = 0.5$ yields $\Delta = -0.98$\,pp (SC $= 58.33\%$), $T = 0.7$ yields $\Delta = +3.43$\,pp (SC $= 55.88\%$), and $T = 1.0$ yields $\Delta = 0.00$\,pp (SC $= 55.39\%$). All other temperatures yield negative or null effects, and the single positive result at $T = 0.7$ is confounded with SC baseline. Combined with the seed sensitivity (0 of 4 seeds reaching $p < 0.05$ at $T = 0.7$), the positive trend depends on a specific combination of temperature, seed, and baseline accuracy that same-model sampling rarely achieves.
457	
458	The parallel between $K$ and $T$ sensitivity points to a common underlying variable. In both sweeps, the setting that produces the largest SICA gain also produces a relatively low SC baseline (55.88\%), which places it in the intermediate-SC region. The hyperparameters themselves ($K$, $T$) are not the causal driver; they modulate SC level, and it is the SC level that determines whether SICA has operating room.
459	
460

464

489	
490	Two patterns emerge. First, low-entropy problems produce $\Delta \approx 0$\,pp across all eight conditions: when traces agree, constraints carry no information beyond the majority vote.
491	
492	Second, the direction of SICA's effect on high-entropy problems depends on the accuracy regime, not on entropy alone. In the intermediate-SC region (Mistral-7B on FOLIO, seeds 42 and 123), high-entropy $\Delta$ reaches $+12.8$ and $+9.1$\,pp: traces genuinely disagree, and constraints from correct-answer traces provide discriminative signal that resolves the disagreement. In Type~A conditions (Qwen2.5-14B on FOLIO, $\Delta = -8.2$\,pp), high-entropy problems represent rare deviations from a strong model's consensus; constraints inherit the model's confirmation bias. In Type~C (Mistral-7B on ProofWriter, $\Delta = -4.9$\,pp), high-entropy problems reflect systematic errors. The mechanism is not entropy per se but the interaction of entropy with model capability.
493	
494	
495	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
496	\label{app:failure-modes}
497	\label{sec:failure-modes}
498	
499	The regime-dependent pattern in Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reflects three distinct failure modes of self-extracted symbolic constraints.
500	
501	\paragraph{Type A: confirmation bias (SC $\geq$ $\sim$60\%).} Strong models produce consistent reasoning traces, and constraints extracted from those traces mirror the answer distribution. When most constraints support the same candidate, MAX-SAT excludes few clauses ($|\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}| \approx 0$), the penalty term in Eq.~\ref{eq:score} vanishes, and the scoring function degenerates to weighted vote counting (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). SICA and SC then produce identical predictions. The ablation in Appendix~\ref{app:ablation} confirms this empirically: on Qwen2.5-14B (FOLIO + ProofWriter, $n = 804$), MAX-SAT and count-only baselines agree on 803 of 804 predictions.
502	
503	\paragraph{Type B: non-formalizable domains.} LogiQA problems require pragmatic and contextual reasoning that resists clean FOL formalization. Qwen2.5-14B on LogiQA achieves an extraction rate of 86.4\%, but the resulting constraints have a contradiction rate of only 0.8\%: extracted formulas rarely conflict across traces, leaving MAX-SAT with no logical tension to exploit. Mistral-7B on LogiQA sits in the same SC range (51.00\%) yet produces $\Delta = +0.50$\,pp ($p = 1.0$); Qwen2.5-14B on the same domain yields $\Delta = -0.50$\,pp ($p = 1.0$). Neither model benefits, confirming that the right accuracy regime is necessary but not sufficient when the domain resists formalization.
504	
505	\paragraph{Type C: error amplification (SC $\leq$ $\sim$40\%).} When model accuracy is low, the majority of traces reach incorrect answers. Constraints extracted from wrong traces encode incorrect reasoning as formal rules. Z3 treats these rules as soft clauses with weight proportional to their frequency, so the solver preferentially satisfies the wrong constraints and penalizes traces that happen to reach the correct answer. Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($\Delta = -2.83$\,pp, $p = 0.014$) demonstrates this effect: SICA converts a weak positive signal in trace diversity into a net negative by formalizing the errors.
506	
507

Same-model methods cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$ (gray band), while cross-model SC shows stronger correlations ($\kappa = 0.41$--$0.74$) at higher accuracy. 517	
518	\section{Cross-Model Kappa Analysis}
519	\label{app:cross-model-kappa}
520	
521	Table~\ref{tab:cross_model_kappa} compares trace independence across three model families on the same benchmarks. On FOLIO ($n = 204$), all four models produce $\kappa \geq 0.41$: LLaMA-3.1-8B ($\kappa = 0.41$), Mistral-7B ($\kappa = 0.44$), Qwen2.5-7B ($\kappa = 0.66$), and Qwen2.5-14B ($\kappa = 0.74$). The bottleneck is architecture-independent---no family escapes the correlation floor. Within the Qwen family, $\kappa$ increases from 0.66 (7B) to 0.74 (14B), but Mistral-7B (7B) and LLaMA-3.1-8B (8B) differ by $\Delta\kappa = 0.03$ despite belonging to different families, suggesting that cross-family variation ($\Delta\kappa$ up to 0.22 at similar scale) exceeds within-family scale effects ($\Delta\kappa = 0.08$). Disentangling scale from architecture requires controlled same-family experiments across $\geq$3 scales.

543	\section{Constraint Quality Across Methods}
544	\label{app:constraint-quality}
545	
546	Table~\ref{tab:constraint_quality} compares constraint quality metrics across the three method-level interventions on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$). Standard SICA extracts from reasoning traces and achieves the highest parse rate (97.1\%) but also the highest redundancy (42.9\%): nearly half of all constraints are syntactic duplicates across the 12 traces, quantifying the co-sourcing effect of same-model generation. ICG generates constraints directly from the problem statement without trace conditioning. Its lower parse rate (74.6\%) reflects the difficulty of standalone formalization at the 7B scale, though successfully parsed constraints compile at a higher rate (99.4\% vs.\ 96.5\%). ICG produces fewer unique constraints per question (24.9 vs.\ 39.3) with lower redundancy (32.3\%), consistent with the absence of trace-level repetition. Contrastive scoring generates pro and con constraint sets for each candidate answer. The format yields far fewer unique constraints per question (9.2) with zero redundancy (each pro/con pair is structurally distinct), but 62.7\% of constraints are trivially satisfied by Z3---they impose no logical tension and cannot discriminate between candidates. Only 51.0\% of Contrastive constraints are discriminative, compared to 81.4\% for standard SICA and 77.0\% for ICG.

All produce neutral-to-negative results, completing the exhaustive remediation analysis across 22+ distinct approaches.}\n\\label{tab:additional_interventions}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llllrl@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\# & Method & Type & Target & $\\Delta$ (pp) & Key Finding \\\\\n\\midrule\n13 & PROVE (code verif.) & Paradigm & Formal proof & $-$1.9 & 15.2\\% compilable; conclusive acc.\\ = 50\\% \\\\\n14 & CEGIS (iterative) & Multi-round & Consistency & 0.0$^\\dagger$ & R1 $+$3.43\\,pp then oscillates \\\\\n15 & DeepConf (logprob) & Filtering & Confidence & 0.0 & $\\Delta_{\\text{logprob}} = 0.006$ (no separation) \\\\\n16 & Cross-model SICA & Cross-model & Independence & $-$0.49 & $\\kappa_{\\text{cross}}{=}0.42$ vs $\\kappa_{\\text{within}}{=}0.53$ \\\\\n17 & Problem perturbation & Input diversity & Trace diversity & $<$0 & $\\kappa$ \\emph{increased} to 0.70 (anti-effect) \\\\\n18 & MIN-UNSAT & Solver objective & Constraint sel.\\ & $-$6.5 & Mean excluded constraints $= 1.3$ \\\\\n19 & $\\kappa$-aware SC & Voting rule & Weighted voting & 0.0 & 11/11 conditions $\\Delta = 0$ \\\\\n20 & Z3 trace filter & Trace filtering & Quality & $-$0.49 & Precision 49.1\\%, 7 questions affected \\\\\n21 & Bootstrapped gold & Iterative & Self-improvement & 0.0$^\\dagger$ & R1 $+$2.45\\,pp then oscillates \\\\\n22 & Selective abstention & Diagnostic & Confidence & $+$4.4$^*$ & AUROC $= 0.673$, at 80\\% coverage \\\\\n23 & ASCII perturbation & Input diversity & Format diversity & 0.0 & $\\kappa$: $0.39 \\to 0.36$ (negligible) \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\\\\[2pt]\n{\\footnotesize All experiments on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n{=}204$) unless noted. $^\\dagger$\\,Oscillates without convergence; net effect $\\approx 0$. $^*$\\,Accuracy gain only at reduced coverage (diagnostic value, not a general improvement). The grey band indicates $\\pm 1$\\,pp noise zone.}\n\\label{fig:interventions-summary}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\n\\section{Extraction Error Taxonomy}\n\\label{app:extraction}\n\\label{sec:extraction-taxonomy}\n\nConstraints must discriminate between candidate answers for MAX-SAT to produce a ranking different from majority vote. We measure discriminative power at two levels on Qwen2.5-14B across FOLIO and ProofWriter ($n = 804$). At the source-trace level, 39.2\\% of total constraint weight originates from traces whose answer differs from the plurality, but only 12.1\\% of individual constraints fall into this minority category. At the Z3 semantic level, 91.8\\% of constraints [95\\% CI: 91.2, 92.4] are technically discriminative, producing different satisfiability outcomes when evaluated against different candidate answers. Only 8.2\\% are non-discriminative.\n\nThe high discriminative rate does not translate into accuracy gains because discrimination mirrors the model's own answer distribution. Each trace produces constraints with its own variable names, so Z3 treats identically-intended propositions as unrelated symbols; among cross-answer constraint pairs, 56.4\\% share zero Z3 variables. This variable isolation means constraints from different traces are trivially jointly satisfiable, and MAX-SAT retains nearly all of them regardless of source answer. Removing the 8.2\\% non-discriminative constraints yields zero accuracy improvement. The discriminative majority does not provide independent signal; it reinforces the majority answer that SC would already select. This confirmation bias, not a lack of discrimination, explains why SICA degenerates to majority voting (Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation}).\n\n\n\\section{Hyperparameter Sensitivity}\n\\label{app:sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:k-sensitivity}\n\\label{sec:t-sensitivity}\n\n\\subsection{K Sensitivity}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:k-sensitivity} shows SICA $\\Delta$ as a function of the number of sampled traces $K$ for Mistral-7B on FOLIO ($n = 204$, seed 42). The relationship is non-monotonic: $K = 4$ yields $\\Delta = -1.47$\\,pp, $K = 8$ yields $\\Delta = -1.96$\\,pp, $K = 12$ reaches $\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp, $K = 16$ drops to $\\Delta = +1.47$\\,pp, and $K = 20$ partially recovers to $\\Delta = +1.96$\\,pp. The recovery at $K = 20$ rules out a simple peaked pattern, but $K = 12$ remains the only setting with $\\Delta > 2$\\,pp ($p = 0.092$, marginal).\n\nThe apparent $\\Delta$ peak at $K = 12$ is confounded with SC baseline. SICA absolute accuracy plateaus at 59.31\\% (121/204) for $K \\geq 12$: identical at $K = 12$ and $K = 20$. The $\\Delta$ variation across these settings is driven primarily by SC fluctuations rather than by SICA improvement. $K = 12$ produces a relatively low SC (55.88\\%), placing it in the intermediate-SC region; $K = 8$ (SC $= 58.33\\%$) and $K = 20$ (SC $= 57.35\\%$) have higher baselines, compressing $\\Delta$ even though SICA accuracy does not change for $K \\geq 12$. Additional traces beyond $K = 12$ provide no benefit to SICA absolute performance, consistent with the constraint quality bottleneck. The $K = 12$ $\\Delta$ captures only 24.1\\% of the oracle ceiling ($+14.22$\\,pp).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{f3_k_sensitivity}\n\\caption{SICA $\\Delta$ (pp) vs.\\ number of traces $K$ for Mistral-7B on FOLIO ($n = 204$, seed 42, $T = 0.7$). Non-monotonic pattern: SICA accuracy plateaus at 59.31\\% for $K \\geq 12$; the apparent $\\Delta$ peak at $K = 12$ partly reflects a relatively low SC baseline (55.88\\%) at that point. Error bars: 95\\% bootstrap CI.}\n\\label{fig:k-sensitivity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Temperature Sensitivity}\n\nTemperature sensitivity on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$, seed 42) reveals a sharp isolated peak rather than a smooth trend. Across four temperature settings, only $T = 0.7$ produces a positive effect: $T = 0.3$ yields $\\Delta = -1.47$\\,pp (SC $= 58.33\\%$), $T = 0.5$ yields $\\Delta = -0.98$\\,pp (SC $= 58.33\\%$), $T = 0.7$ yields $\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp (SC $= 55.88\\%$), and $T = 1.0$ yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp (SC $= 55.39\\%$). All other temperatures yield negative or null effects, and the single positive result at $T = 0.7$ is confounded with SC baseline. Combined with the seed sensitivity (0 of 4 seeds reaching $p < 0.05$ at $T = 0.7$), the positive trend depends on a specific combination of temperature, seed, and baseline accuracy that same-model sampling rarely achieves.\n\nThe parallel between $K$ and $T$ sensitivity points to a common underlying variable. In both sweeps, the setting that produces the largest SICA gain also produces a relatively low SC baseline (55.88\\%), which places it in the intermediate-SC region. The hyperparameters themselves ($K$, $T$) are not the causal driver; they modulate SC level, and it is the SC level that determines whether SICA has operating room.\n\n\n\\section{Entropy Decomposition}\n\\label{app:entropy}\n\\label{sec:entropy}\n\nWe partition problems by trace agreement: high-entropy (majority count $\\leq 8$ out of $K = 12$) and low-entropy (majority count $\\geq 9$). Table~\\ref{tab:entropy} reports SICA $\\Delta$ within each partition across eight model-dataset conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Entropy decomposition of SICA $\\Delta$ (pp). High-entropy: majority count $\\leq 8$; low-entropy: majority count $\\geq 9$. $^\\dagger$Partial data (180/600). Pre-tiebreaking SC values; pattern robust to D064 correction.}\n\\label{tab:entropy}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Dataset & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{High-entropy} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{Low-entropy} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6}\n & & $n$ & $\\Delta$ & $n$ & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nMistral-7B (s42) & FOLIO & 78 & $\\mathbf{+12.8}$ & 126 & 0.0 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 77 & $\\mathbf{+9.1}$ & 127 & 0.0 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 75 & $-$1.3 & 129 & 0.0 \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 267 & $-$4.9 & 333 & $-$1.2 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 37 & $-$8.2 & 167 & $-$0.6 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 23 & $-$4.3 & 177 & 0.0 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 47 & $-$2.1 & 157 & 0.0 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B$^\\dagger$ & PW-D5 & 76 & $-$3.9 & 104 & $-$1.9 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nTwo patterns emerge. First, low-entropy problems produce $\\Delta \\approx 0$\\,pp across all eight conditions: when traces agree, constraints carry no information beyond the majority vote.\n\nSecond, the direction of SICA's effect on high-entropy problems depends on the accuracy regime, not on entropy alone. In the intermediate-SC region (Mistral-7B on FOLIO, seeds 42 and 123), high-entropy $\\Delta$ reaches $+12.8$ and $+9.1$\\,pp: traces genuinely disagree, and constraints from correct-answer traces provide discriminative signal that resolves the disagreement. In Type~A conditions (Qwen2.5-14B on FOLIO, $\\Delta = -8.2$\\,pp), high-entropy problems represent rare deviations from a strong model's consensus; constraints inherit the model's confirmation bias. In Type~C (Mistral-7B on ProofWriter, $\\Delta = -4.9$\\,pp), high-entropy problems reflect systematic errors. The mechanism is not entropy per se but the interaction of entropy with model capability.\n\n\n\\section{Failure Mode Analysis}\n\\label{app:failure-modes}\n\\label{sec:failure-modes}\n\nThe regime-dependent pattern in Table~\\ref{tab:main_results} reflects three distinct failure modes of self-extracted symbolic constraints.\n\n\\paragraph{Type A: confirmation bias (SC $\\geq$ $\\sim$60\\%).} Strong models produce consistent reasoning traces, and constraints extracted from those traces mirror the answer distribution. When most constraints support the same candidate, MAX-SAT excludes few clauses ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| \\approx 0$), the penalty term in Eq.~\\ref{eq:score} vanishes, and the scoring function degenerates to weighted vote counting (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). SICA and SC then produce identical predictions. The ablation in Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation} confirms this empirically: on Qwen2.5-14B (FOLIO + ProofWriter, $n = 804$), MAX-SAT and count-only baselines agree on 803 of 804 predictions.\n\n\\paragraph{Type B: non-formalizable domains.} LogiQA problems require pragmatic and contextual reasoning that resists clean FOL formalization. Qwen2.5-14B on LogiQA achieves an extraction rate of 86.4\\%, but the resulting constraints have a contradiction rate of only 0.8\\%: extracted formulas rarely conflict across traces, leaving MAX-SAT with no logical tension to exploit. Mistral-7B on LogiQA sits in the same SC range (51.00\\%) yet produces $\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp ($p = 1.0$); Qwen2.5-14B on the same domain yields $\\Delta = -0.50$\\,pp ($p = 1.0$). Neither model benefits, confirming that the right accuracy regime is necessary but not sufficient when the domain resists formalization.\n\n\\paragraph{Type C: error amplification (SC $\\leq$ $\\sim$40\\%).} When model accuracy is low, the majority of traces reach incorrect answers. Constraints extracted from wrong traces encode incorrect reasoning as formal rules. Z3 treats these rules as soft clauses with weight proportional to their frequency, so the solver preferentially satisfies the wrong constraints and penalizes traces that happen to reach the correct answer. Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($\\Delta = -2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$) demonstrates this effect: SICA converts a weak positive signal in trace diversity into a net negative by formalizing the errors.\n\n\n\\section{Independence-Accuracy Scatter Plot}\n\\label{app:kappa-scatter}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/f6_kappa_accuracy_scatter}\n\\caption{Independence-accuracy trade-off across aggregation methods (Mistral-7B, filled markers) and model families (LLaMA-3.1-8B, Qwen2.5-7B, Qwen2.5-14B; open markers) on FOLIO ($n = 204$). Same-model methods cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$ (gray band), while cross-model SC shows stronger correlations ($\\kappa = 0.41$--$0.74$) at higher accuracy. Debate breaks correlation ($\\kappa = 0.15$) at catastrophic accuracy cost. Dashed line: Mistral-7B SC baseline.}\n\\label{fig:kappa-accuracy}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Cross-Model Kappa Analysis}\n\\label{app:cross-model-kappa}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_model_kappa} compares trace independence across three model families on the same benchmarks. On FOLIO ($n = 204$), all four models produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$: LLaMA-3.1-8B ($\\kappa = 0.41$), Mistral-7B ($\\kappa = 0.44$), Qwen2.5-7B ($\\kappa = 0.66$), and Qwen2.5-14B ($\\kappa = 0.74$). The bottleneck is architecture-independent---no family escapes the correlation floor. Within the Qwen family, $\\kappa$ increases from 0.66 (7B) to 0.74 (14B), but Mistral-7B (7B) and LLaMA-3.1-8B (8B) differ by $\\Delta\\kappa = 0.03$ despite belonging to different families, suggesting that cross-family variation ($\\Delta\\kappa$ up to 0.22 at similar scale) exceeds within-family scale effects ($\\Delta\\kappa = 0.08$). Disentangling scale from architecture requires controlled same-family experiments across $\\geq$3 scales.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-model trace independence ($K = 12$, SC aggregation, FOLIO + ProofWriter). Higher $\\kappa$ indicates stronger correlation and lower effective sample size.}\n\\label{tab:cross_model_kappa}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Dataset & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.32 & 75.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.44 & 2.02 & 56.86 \\\\\nLLaMA-3.1-8B & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 66.18 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQwen2.5-14B & PW-D5 & 0.62 & 1.54 & --- \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Constraint Quality Across Methods}\n\\label{app:constraint-quality}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:constraint_quality} compares constraint quality metrics across the three method-level interventions on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$). Standard SICA extracts from reasoning traces and achieves the highest parse rate (97.1\\%) but also the highest redundancy (42.9\\%): nearly half of all constraints are syntactic duplicates across the 12 traces, quantifying the co-sourcing effect of same-model generation. ICG generates constraints directly from the problem statement without trace conditioning. Its lower parse rate (74.6\\%) reflects the difficulty of standalone formalization at the 7B scale, though successfully parsed constraints compile at a higher rate (99.4\\% vs.\\ 96.5\\%). ICG produces fewer unique constraints per question (24.9 vs.\\ 39.3) with lower redundancy (32.3\\%), consistent with the absence of trace-level repetition. Contrastive scoring generates pro and con constraint sets for each candidate answer. The format yields far fewer unique constraints per question (9.2) with zero redundancy (each pro/con pair is structurally distinct), but 62.7\\% of constraints are trivially satisfied by Z3---they impose no logical tension and cannot discriminate between candidates. Only 51.0\\% of Contrastive constraints are discriminative, compared to 81.4\\% for standard SICA and 77.0\\% for ICG.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering",

564	
565	\section{Error Categorization of Method-Level Interventions}
566	\label{app:error-categorization}
567	
568	We categorize discordant predictions (where the method and SC disagree) for Debate and Contrastive on FOLIO ($n = 204$) to understand why these interventions degrade accuracy.
569	
570	\paragraph{Debate discordant analysis.} Debate produces 60 correct$\to$wrong flips and 22 wrong$\to$correct flips on FOLIO. Answer-type breakdown reveals that False-labeled questions are most vulnerable: 82.8\% of False questions that SC answers correctly are flipped by Debate, compared to 63.9\% for True and 25.5\% for Unknown. Counterintuitively, failures concentrate not where debate breaks down but where it succeeds most thoroughly: discordant questions average 2.93 effective debate rounds (proposer-critic exchanges producing substantive updates) versus 2.52 for concordant ones. More extensive same-model debate produces more confident but incorrect constraints, consistent with the independence bottleneck---the critic draws on the same model distribution as the proposer, reinforcing shared biases rather than correcting them.
571	
572	\paragraph{Contrastive discordant analysis.} Contrastive produces 34 discordant questions on FOLIO. All 34 produced non-degenerate scores (both pro and con constraint sets non-empty), compared to 20.1\% degenerate scores in the full sample. This indicates that scoring failures on discordant questions stem from systematic pro-bias rather than missing constraints. Among discordant questions, 97.1\% contain negation words in the problem statement versus 76.5\% in the full sample, suggesting that negation-bearing problems are particularly susceptible to pro-constraint imbalance. The average pro/con score ratio is 1.0 for discordant questions versus 0.68 for the full sample: the method generates balanced-looking scores that mask directional errors.
573	
574	\paragraph{Cross-method overlap.} Of the 79 unique questions on which either method disagrees with SC, only 15 overlap between Debate and Contrastive (Jaccard index $= 0.19$). All 15 shared discordant questions contain negation words. The low overlap confirms that the two methods attack different vulnerability types---Debate through extended same-model argumentation that amplifies confident errors, Contrastive through pro-bias in constraint generation---while sharing a common root cause in the independence bottleneck: both methods rely on the same model's output distribution and cannot introduce genuinely independent signal.
575	
576	
577	\section{Extended Limitations}
578	\label{app:limitations-extended}
579	
580	Beyond the limitations discussed in the main text, several additional constraints bound our conclusions. Seven of our eleven model-domain conditions have $n \leq 60$, providing insufficient statistical power for confirmatory claims; power analysis shows that the minimum detectable effect exceeds 5\,pp for these conditions (Table~\ref{tab:power}), so they serve as regime characterization rather than definitive tests. Our extraction prompts use a single zero-shot template per domain; alternative prompting strategies (few-shot exemplars, chain-of-thought extraction, or iterative refinement) might yield higher-quality constraints, though the oracle gap suggests the bottleneck is fundamental rather than prompt-specific. The constraint quality analysis (91.8\% discriminative rate, 39.2\% source-trace minority weight) covers Qwen2.5-14B on FOLIO + ProofWriter ($N = 804$); other model-domain combinations may exhibit different characteristics. Constraint extraction uses $T_{\text{ext}} = 0.3$ while trace sampling uses $T = 0.7$; alternative extraction temperatures might yield different results. Although we prompt for FOL-style formalizations, extracted constraints are predominantly propositional ground clauses without quantifiers (\S\ref{sec:constraint-extraction}); whether models capable of genuine quantified FOL would yield higher discriminative rates is untested. Deduplication uses syntactic equivalence, so semantically equivalent constraints with different surface forms receive separate weights. The GSM8K pilot ($n = 30$) illustrates the constraint-absent regime and should not be interpreted as confirmatory evidence.
581

Triv.\\ Sat.: fraction trivially satisfied (no logical tension).}\n\\label{tab:constraint_quality}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}l rrrrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Parse & Z3 & Uniq/q & Redund. & Disc. & Triv.\\ Sat. \\\\\n\\midrule\nStandard SICA & 97.1\\% & 96.5\\% & 39.3 & 42.9\\% & 81.4\\% & --- \\\\\nICG & 74.6\\% & 99.4\\% & 24.9 & 32.3\\% & 77.0\\% & --- \\\\\nContrastive & 74.0\\% & 94.4\\% & 9.2 & 0.0\\% & 51.0\\% & 62.7\\% \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\section{Error Categorization of Method-Level Interventions}\n\\label{app:error-categorization}\n\nWe categorize discordant predictions (where the method and SC disagree) for Debate and Contrastive on FOLIO ($n = 204$) to understand why these interventions degrade accuracy.\n\n\\paragraph{Debate discordant analysis.} Debate produces 60 correct$\\to$wrong flips and 22 wrong$\\to$correct flips on FOLIO. Answer-type breakdown reveals that False-labeled questions are most vulnerable: 82.8\\% of False questions that SC answers correctly are flipped by Debate, compared to 63.9\\% for True and 25.5\\% for Unknown. Counterintuitively, failures concentrate not where debate breaks down but where it succeeds most thoroughly: discordant questions average 2.93 effective debate rounds (proposer-critic exchanges producing substantive updates) versus 2.52 for concordant ones. More extensive same-model debate produces more confident but incorrect constraints, consistent with the independence bottleneck---the critic draws on the same model distribution as the proposer, reinforcing shared biases rather than correcting them.\n\n\\paragraph{Contrastive discordant analysis.} Contrastive produces 34 discordant questions on FOLIO. All 34 produced non-degenerate scores (both pro and con constraint sets non-empty), compared to 20.1\\% degenerate scores in the full sample. This indicates that scoring failures on discordant questions stem from systematic pro-bias rather than missing constraints. Among discordant questions, 97.1\\% contain negation words in the problem statement versus 76.5\\% in the full sample, suggesting that negation-bearing problems are particularly susceptible to pro-constraint imbalance. The average pro/con score ratio is 1.0 for discordant questions versus 0.68 for the full sample: the method generates balanced-looking scores that mask directional errors.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-method overlap.} Of the 79 unique questions on which either method disagrees with SC, only 15 overlap between Debate and Contrastive (Jaccard index $= 0.19$). All 15 shared discordant questions contain negation words. The low overlap confirms that the two methods attack different vulnerability types---Debate through extended same-model argumentation that amplifies confident errors, Contrastive through pro-bias in constraint generation---while sharing a common root cause in the independence bottleneck: both methods rely on the same model's output distribution and cannot introduce genuinely independent signal.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations-extended}\n\nBeyond the limitations discussed in the main text, several additional constraints bound our conclusions. Seven of our eleven model-domain conditions have $n \\leq 60$, providing insufficient statistical power for confirmatory claims; power analysis shows that the minimum detectable effect exceeds 5\\,pp for these conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:power}), so they serve as regime characterization rather than definitive tests. [Director 指令] [D090 — Case A 确认，V2 Reframe]

R 结果出了，positive signal 确认。论文 reframe。

## R 核心数据
- DeBERTa-small NLI standalone: 49.51%
- NLI+SC combo (w=3): **58.82% (+4.41pp vs SC)**
- NLI as selective verifier (45.1% coverage): **68.48%**
- 待验证：seed 一致性 + McNemar p-value（Agent 处理中）

## V2 Framing（立即切换）
论文叙事从 "all methods fail" 升级为 **"same-model methods fail, but cross-architecture verification succeeds"**。

核心 story spine 调整：
1. SC 准确率有天花板 → SICA 试图用符号验证突破 → 失败
2. 根因诊断：independence bottleneck + confirmation bias (4.97×)
3. **25+ same-model 修复全败 → bottleneck 是结构性的**
4. **NEW: cross-architecture NLI (DeBERTa) 打破了这个 bottleneck → +4.41pp**
5. 这验证了 oracle gap 的实践意义：外部信号是唯一出路

## 需要改的 sections
1. **Abstract**: 加 cross-architecture positive finding，修正 "nine conditions" → "fifteen non-degenerate conditions" 或 "nine Type A conditions"（Reviewer critical #6）
2. **Intro**: 最后段加 "We demonstrate that..." positive contribution
3. **Method**: 加 §3.X "Cross-Architecture NLI Verification"（~0.3pp，描述 DeBERTa pipeline + combo/verifier 两种模式）
4. **Experiments**: 加 §4.X "Cross-Architecture Verification Results"（Table + analysis，~0.5pp）
5. **Discussion**: 加强 practical implications — "practitioners should consider external lightweight models as verifiers"
6. **Conclusion**: 从纯 negative 改为 "diagnosis + actionable solution path"
7. **Limitations**: 按 Reviewer #22/#23 拆分为 3 子段，补 SICA 计算成本、prompt 敏感性、bias ratio 单条件声明

## Reviewer 审稿 Critical 修复（必须）
- **#6/#25**: Abstract "nine conditions" 与 Table 2 (15 conditions) 不一致 → 修正
- **#12**: Confirmation bias 4.97× 仅 Mistral-7B FOLIO → Limitations 中声明，Agent 同时补跨条件 bias ratio

## Reviewer Warning 优先修复
- **#3**: Method §3.2 混入经验结果 → 改为前瞻引用 "We show in §X that..."
- **#4**: 缺计算成本 → 加一句 wall-time 对比
- **#8**: 4-seed 仅 Mistral FOLIO → Limitations 声明
- **#26**: Conclusion 与 Abstract 重复 → 加前瞻性讨论

## 页数预算
R section 需要 ~0.8pp。当前 8pp 正文。EMNLP long paper = 8pp 限制。需要从其他地方压缩 0.8pp 或把 R 放 appendix 作为 main result 引用。建议方案：压缩 §4.4 Supporting Diagnostics (移部分到 appendix) + §4.5 Remediation Analysis (压缩 table)。

先修 Abstract critical (#6)，再做 V2 reframe。汇报修改计划。

_source line 9873 · 2026-05-20T11:38:20.245Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011NDQUz5PruQhDEPxJd247u`</summary>

```text
1	Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.
2	We investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.
3	Evaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 instances, we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: zero of nine conditions reaches $p < 0.05$, while multiple conditions show significant degradation.
4	We identify \emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\times$), and 94\% of errors overlap with SC.
5	Process-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.
6	More than twenty remediation strategies---spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions---all fail, revealing a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\kappa \geq 0.40$ across three model families).
7	Oracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+14.22$\,pp FOLIO, $+11.50$\,pp ProofWriter, $p < 0.001$); self-extraction captures only 24\% of this ceiling.
8	The bottleneck is structural at the 7B--32B scale and universal across architectures.
```

</details>

    "newString": "none of fifteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect ($p < 0.05$), while two conditions show significant degradation.",
    "originalFile": "Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.\nWe investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts first-order logic (FOL) constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.\nEvaluating six models (7B--32B) across three reasoning domains with over 3,000 instances, we find that self-extracted constraints fail to surpass majority voting: zero of nine conditions reaches $p < 0.05$, while multiple conditions show significant degradation.\nWe identify \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC.\nProcess-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.\nMore than twenty remediation strategies---spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions---all fail, revealing a fundamental independence-accuracy trade-off: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$ across three model families).\nOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+14.22$\\,pp FOLIO, $+11.50$\\,pp ProofWriter, $p < 0.001$); self-extraction captures only 24\\% of this ceiling.\nThe bottleneck is structural at the 7B--32B scale and universal across architectures.",

65	\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}
66	\label{sec:diagnostics}
67	
68	Four analyses confirm the mechanism (details in Appendices~\ref{app:extraction}--\ref{app:entropy}). Extraction taxonomy: 91.8\% of constraints are discriminative, but their direction mirrors the majority; 42.9\% are redundant across traces. Constraint ablation ($n = 804$): MAX-SAT and count-only agree on 94.4\% of predictions---the solver adds no value over counting because constraint weights carry information redundant with vote counts. Hyperparameter sensitivity: $K = 12$ is the only setting with $\Delta > 2$\,pp among five values; $T = 0.7$ the only temperature with a positive effect---peaks coincide with low SC baselines. Entropy decomposition: all effects concentrate in high-entropy problems (up to $+12.8$\,pp), but direction depends on accuracy regime.
69	
70

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PAyHQHAPRDYNn4EoYKsdBn`</summary>

```text
70	
71	\subsection{Remediation Analysis}
72	\label{sec:remediation}
73	\label{sec:interventions}
74	
75	We test more than twenty remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and ten additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.
76	
77	\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail. Contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints from 8.2\% to 6.0\%, but newly discriminative constraints still mirror the majority ($\Delta = -1.1$\,pp). Cross-model extraction fails in both directions: weaker models yield 0\% discriminative constraints; stronger models produce accuracy within $\pm 1$\,pp of self-extraction. Multi-temperature sampling ($T \in \{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\}$) yields $\Delta = 0.00$\,pp.
78	
79	\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} Three strategies target different aspects of the independence bottleneck (Table~\ref{tab:method_interventions}). Independent Constraint Generation (ICG) bypasses trace-constraint coupling by generating constraints directly from premises. Despite producing superior constraints---higher Z3 compilation, lower redundancy---ICG yields $\Delta \approx 0$ across three seeds ($p = .206$, n.s.). Per-constraint-set reranking produces $\kappa = 0.47$, nearly identical to SC's $\kappa = 0.44$: the bottleneck is a property of the model itself. Contrastive scoring yields $\Delta = -9.31$\,pp on FOLIO ($p < 0.01$); 62.7\% of constraints are trivially satisfied. Debate produces $\Delta = -18.63$ and $-23.00$\,pp ($p < 0.001$ on both datasets), with discordant pairs heavily skewed toward correct$\to$wrong flips. Three of six method-dataset conditions reach significance, all negative.
80	
81	\begin{table}[t]
82	\centering
83	\small
84	\caption{Method-level interventions on Mistral-7B with McNemar's paired test. All produce neutral-to-catastrophic results. ICG shows two FOLIO seeds; $p$ for the first. {*}{*}{*}\,$p < 0.001$; {*}{*}\,$p < 0.01$.}
85	\label{tab:method_interventions}
86	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll rc rc@{}}
87	\toprule
88	& & \multicolumn{2}{c}{FOLIO ($n = 204$)} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{LogiQA ($n = 200$)} \\
89	\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-6}
90	Method & Target & $\Delta$ (pp) & $p$ & $\Delta$ (pp) & $p$ \\
91	\midrule
92	SC baseline & --- & 0.00 & --- & 0.00 & --- \\
93	ICG & Trace coupling & $-$1.96\,/\,$+$1.47 & .206 & $-$0.50 & .706 \\
94	Contrastive & Scoring bias & $\mathbf{-9.31}$ & .007 & $-$4.50 & .160 \\
95	Debate & Role diversity & $\mathbf{-18.63}$ & ${<}.001$ & $\mathbf{-23.00}$ & ${<}.001$ \\
96	\bottomrule
97	\end{tabular}
98	\end{table}
99	
100	\paragraph{Additional directions.} Ten further experiments confirm the pattern (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:additional_interventions} and Figure~\ref{fig:interventions-summary}). Formal verification via code generation (PROVE) achieves only 15.2\% compilable programs with $\Delta = -1.9$\,pp. Iterative CEGIS refinement oscillates after round~1 without convergence. Logprob-based filtering (DeepConf) finds near-zero separation between correct and incorrect traces ($\Delta_{\text{logprob}} = 0.006$). Cross-model SICA with three families reduces $\kappa$ from 0.53 to 0.42 but yields $\Delta = -0.49$\,pp ($p = 1.0$). Problem perturbation \emph{increases} $\kappa$ to 0.70 (anti-effect). MIN-UNSAT objective produces $\Delta = -6.5$\,pp. $\kappa$-aware weighted voting yields $\Delta = 0$ across 11 conditions. Z3-based trace filtering achieves 49.1\% precision with $\Delta = -0.49$\,pp. Bootstrapped gold constraints oscillate like CEGIS. ASCII format perturbation yields negligible $\kappa$ reduction (0.39 $\to$ 0.36).
101	
102	\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} compares Fleiss' $\kappa$ across four aggregation methods on Mistral-7B FOLIO and adds SC from three model families. SC ($\kappa = 0.44$), Diverse-Prompt ($\kappa = 0.40$), and ICG ($\kappa = 0.47$) cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$, forming a same-model ceiling. Debate breaks through to $\kappa = 0.15$ ($K_{\text{eff}} = 4.44$) but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\,pp. All three model families cluster above $\kappa = 0.40$, confirming the ceiling is architecture-independent (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}). This constitutes a Pareto frontier (Figure~\ref{fig:kappa-accuracy}): methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy, while methods that preserve accuracy cannot break the correlation floor. Cross-model SICA (Mistral+LLaMA+Qwen, $K{=}12$) provides direct evidence: mixing three families reduces $\kappa$ from 0.53 to 0.42 yet yields $\Delta = -0.49$\,pp ($p = 1.0$)---even cross-architecture diversity is insufficient within the self-extraction paradigm. The only demonstrated path past this frontier requires signal from outside the model's distribution, consistent with the oracle result (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) and published systems that bypass self-extraction \citep{xu2026dylos, li2025aristotle, wang2025fover, pan2023logiclm}.
103	
104	\begin{table}[t]
105	\centering
106	\small
107	\caption{Independence-accuracy trade-off (FOLIO, $n = 204$). Top: aggregation methods on Mistral-7B; bottom: SC across three model families. All families produce $\kappa \geq 0.41$, confirming an architecture-independent ceiling. $\kappa$: Fleiss' kappa on raw answers.}
108	\label{tab:kappa_generalization}
109	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}
110	\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}
111	\toprule
112	Model & Aggregation & $\kappa$ & Eff-$K$ & $K$ & Acc.\ (\%) \\
113	\midrule
114	Mistral-7B & Debate & 0.15 & 4.44 & 12 & 38.24 \\
115	& Diverse-Prompt & 0.40 & 2.21 & 12 & 54.41 \\
116	& Self-Consistency & 0.44 & 2.02 & 10 & 56.86 \\
117	& ICG & 0.47 & 1.93 & 10 & 51.96 \\
118	\addlinespace
119	LLaMA-3.1-8B & Self-Consistency & 0.41 & 2.17 & 12 & 66.18 \\
120	Qwen2.5-7B & Self-Consistency & 0.66 & 1.46 & 12 & 75.98 \\
121	Qwen2.5-14B & Self-Consistency & 0.74 & 1.32 & 12 & 75.49 \\
122	\bottomrule
123	\end{tabular}
124	\end{table}

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} compares Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods on Mistral-7B FOLIO and adds SC from three model families. SC ($\\kappa = 0.44$), Diverse-Prompt ($\\kappa = 0.40$), and ICG ($\\kappa = 0.47$) cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$, forming a same-model ceiling. Debate breaks through to $\\kappa = 0.15$ ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 4.44$) but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All three model families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, confirming the ceiling is architecture-independent (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}). This constitutes a Pareto frontier (Figure~\\ref{fig:kappa-accuracy}): methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy, while methods that preserve accuracy cannot break the correlation floor. Cross-model SICA (Mistral+LLaMA+Qwen, $K{=}12$) provides direct evidence: mixing three families reduces $\\kappa$ from 0.53 to 0.42 yet yields $\\Delta = -0.49$\\,pp ($p = 1.0$)---even cross-architecture diversity is insufficient within the self-extraction paradigm. The only demonstrated path past this frontier requires signal from outside the model's distribution, consistent with the oracle result (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) and published systems that bypass self-extraction \\citep{xu2026dylos, li2025aristotle, wang2025fover, pan2023logiclm}.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} compares Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across four same-model aggregation methods and three model families (Figure~\\ref{fig:kappa-accuracy}). SC ($\\kappa = 0.44$), Diverse-Prompt ($\\kappa = 0.40$), and ICG ($\\kappa = 0.47$) cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All three families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}), forming a same-model Pareto frontier: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor. The only demonstrated path past this frontier requires signal from outside the model's distribution (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}), consistent with the oracle result (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) and published systems that bypass self-extraction \\citep{xu2026dylos, li2025aristotle, wang2025fover, pan2023logiclm}."

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01EiK5Npn6Twjs9rZxWc1Ah9`</summary>

```text
36	
37	\paragraph{Degeneration under confirmation bias.} Self-extracted constraints are tightly coupled to their source answer. While ${\sim}$92\% are technically discriminative (Appendix~\ref{app:extraction}), their direction mirrors the model's answer distribution. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all ($|\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}| \approx 0$), and scoring degenerates to:
38	\begin{equation}
39	\label{eq:degenerate}
40	\text{score}(a) \approx \sum_{\substack{c \in \mathcal{C}^* \\ \text{source}(c) \to a}} w_c,
41	\end{equation}
42	equivalent to majority voting. The ablation in Appendix~\ref{app:ablation} confirms this empirically.
43	
44	\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0$ (fraction of questions with $>1$ distinct answer) is a zero-cost diagnostic computable before extraction. When $C_0 < 5\%$, SICA produces the same output as SC.
45	\begin{equation}
46	\label{eq:c0-def}
47	C_0 = \frac{|\{q \in \mathcal{Q} : |\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\}|}{|\mathcal{Q}|}.
48	\end{equation}
49	
50	\begin{figure}[t]
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Self-Consistency and the Condorcet Independence Assumption}\n\\label{sec:condorcet}\n\nSelf-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc} samples $K$ reasoning traces and selects the majority answer. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees that if $K$ independent voters are each correct with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth as $K \\to \\infty$ \\citep{austensmith1996}. LLM traces violate this independence: all $K$ samples come from the same model with only temperature as variation. Following \\citet{ladha1992correlated} and the design-effect framework of \\citet{kish1965survey}, we define effective sample size using Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa} as proxy for intraclass correlation:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:effk}\nK_{\\text{eff}} = \\frac{K}{1 + (K - 1) \\cdot \\kappa}.\n\\end{equation}\nIn our experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82 across instruction-tuned pairs (0.91 for thinking mode), yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} \\approx 1.2\\text{--}3.6$ for $K = 12$. This \\emph{independence bottleneck} motivates our investigation.\n\n\\subsection{SICA Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:pipeline}\n\nSymbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) extends SC by extracting formal constraints from traces and selecting the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\n\n\\paragraph{Trace generation.} Given question $q$, an LLM generates $K$ traces $\\{t_1, \\ldots, t_K\\}$ at temperature $T = 0.7$. Each trace $t_i$ yields answer $a_i$.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint extraction.}\\label{sec:constraint-extraction} The same LLM extracts FOL constraints from each trace at $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$. The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompts}). The \\emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence; each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces. The objective finds the largest-weight satisfiable subset:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:maxsat}\n\\mathcal{C}^* = \\arg\\max_{\\mathcal{S} \\subseteq \\mathcal{C}} \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{S}} w_c \\quad \\text{s.t.} \\quad \\mathcal{S} \\text{ is satisfiable.}\n\\end{equation}\nZ3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} solves this with a 10-second timeout. Answer assertion constraints ($\\texttt{answer\\_a} == \\texttt{True}$, weight 1) ensure every candidate receives a nonzero base score, making SICA a strict generalization of SC.\n\n\\paragraph{Scoring.} Let $\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus} = \\mathcal{C} \\setminus \\mathcal{C}^*$. Each candidate $a$ is scored by:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:score}\n\\text{score}(a) = \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^*} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a) - \\alpha \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if $c$ was extracted from any trace producing answer $a$, and $\\alpha = 0.5$. The selected answer is $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a} \\text{score}(a)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Degeneration under confirmation bias.} Self-extracted constraints are tightly coupled to their source answer. While ${\\sim}$92\\% are technically discriminative (Appendix~\\ref{app:extraction}), their direction mirrors the model's answer distribution. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| \\approx 0$), and scoring degenerates to:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:degenerate}\n\\text{score}(a) \\approx \\sum_{\\substack{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^* \\\\ \\text{source}(c) \\to a}} w_c,\n\\end{equation}\nequivalent to majority voting. The ablation in Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation} confirms this empirically.\n\n\\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0$ (fraction of questions with $>1$ distinct answer) is a zero-cost diagnostic computable before extraction. When $C_0 < 5\\%$, SICA produces the same output as SC.\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:c0-def}\nC_0 = \\frac{|\\{q \\in \\mathcal{Q} : |\\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\\}|}{|\\mathcal{Q}|}.\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n    node distance=0.4cm and 0.35cm,\n    box/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, minimum height=0.6cm,\n                text width=1.6cm, align=center, font=\\scriptsize},\n    arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick},\n    darr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick, densely dashed},\n    lbl/.style={font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!70!black},\n    ibottle/.style={draw=red!70!black, densely dashed, rounded corners=3pt,\n                    inner sep=3pt},\n]\n\\node[box] (q) {Question $q$};\n\\node[box, right=of q] (sample) {Sample $K$\\\\traces};\n\\node[box, right=of sample] (extract) {Extract FOL\\\\constraints};\n\\node[box, right=of extract] (dedup) {Dedup.\\ +\\\\weight};\n\\node[box, right=of dedup] (maxsat) {MAX-SAT\\\\(Z3)};\n\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of maxsat, fill=gray!12] (ans) {Answer $a^*$};\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of sample, fill=gray!12] (sc) {Majority\\\\vote};\n\n\\draw[arr] (q) -- (sample);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- (extract);\n\\draw[arr] (extract) -- (dedup);\n\\draw[arr] (dedup) -- (maxsat);\n\\draw[arr] (maxsat) -- (ans);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- node[lbl, right, yshift=0pt] {SC} (sc);\n\n\\node[lbl, above=0.01cm of maxsat] {SICA};\n\n\\node[ibottle, fit=(sample)(extract), label={[font=\\scriptsize\\itshape, text=red!70!black]above:Independence Bottleneck}] {};\n\n\\draw[darr, gray!60] ([xshift=-0.1cm]dedup.south) -- ++(0,-0.35) node[lbl, below, yshift=-0.05cm] {oracle: gold FOL};\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n\\caption{The SICA pipeline. SC selects the majority answer from $K$ traces. SICA extracts FOL constraints, deduplicates, and solves MAX-SAT via Z3. Red dashed box: the independence bottleneck. Dashed arrow: oracle variant with gold FOL.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Oracle Control Design}\n\\label{sec:oracle-design}\n\nTo isolate whether the bottleneck lies in extraction or aggregation, we introduce an oracle control, following the compositionality-gap methodology of \\citet{press2023compositionality}. Gold FOL annotations (FOLIO) or DSL rules (ProofWriter) replace self-extracted constraints; Z3 evaluates them against each candidate answer, with scoring unchanged (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}). If the oracle produces large gains while self-extraction does not, the failure localizes to extraction quality. Results appear in \\S\\ref{sec:oracle}.\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa-method}\n\nAnswer-level Fleiss' $\\kappa$ may underestimate the true independence bottleneck. Two traces that follow nearly identical reasoning can reach different final answers; $\\kappa$ treats such pairs as disagreeing, overestimating independence. We quantify process-level similarity via pairwise cosine similarity over TF-IDF representations of chain-of-thought traces. For problem $q$ with $K$ traces:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\bar{s}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i, \\mathbf{v}_j),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$. When $\\bar{s} \\gg \\kappa$, reasoning processes are more homogeneous than answers suggest. This metric applies to any trace aggregation method, not only SICA.\n\n\\subsection{Scope}\n\\label{sec:scope}\n\nSICA uses \\emph{same-model self-extraction}: the same LLM generates traces and extracts constraints. We test more than twenty remediation strategies: nine prompt-level spanning six dimensions, three method-level (ICG, Contrastive, Debate), and ten additional directions targeting the independence bottleneck from different angles (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}). Methods using trained extractors, external provers, or multi-model ensembles fall outside scope.",

```text
40	
41	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
42	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
43	
44	The correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\times$ compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal.
45	
46	The 12 instances where SICA corrects SC errors reveal the mechanism's boundary: 43\% of these flips occur when a minority trace provides gold-aligned constraints, suggesting constraint diversity---not MAX-SAT---drives the rare successes. The causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with that answer; aggregation over such constraints reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}).
47	
48	Iterative refinement does not break this cycle. CEGIS achieves $\Delta = +3.43$\,pp in round~1 but oscillates in rounds~2--5 without convergence, as each step reintroduces model bias. Cross-model extraction (Qwen3-14B or Qwen2.5-14B extracting from Mistral-7B traces) produces accuracy within $\pm 1$\,pp of self-extraction---the bias is a property of LLM formalization itself. The oracle ceiling (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that 75.9\% of the potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality, localizing confirmation bias as the binding bottleneck.
49	
50	
51	\subsection{Process-Level Independence}
52	\label{sec:process-kappa}
53	
54	Answer-level $\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), the mean pairwise cosine similarity of chain-of-thought traces (TF-IDF, Eq.~\ref{eq:process-sim}) is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.41$. These measures are uncorrelated ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$): problems with diverse answers do not exhibit diverse reasoning. We find 9.3\% of trace pairs converge on reasoning ($\cos > 0.8$) while disagreeing on the final answer---apparent answer diversity masking process-level homogeneity.
```

```text
89	\subsection{Oracle Control Design}
90	\label{sec:oracle-design}
91	
92	To isolate whether the bottleneck lies in extraction or aggregation, we introduce an oracle control, following the compositionality-gap methodology of \citet{press2023compositionality}. Gold FOL annotations (FOLIO) or DSL rules (ProofWriter) replace self-extracted constraints; Z3 evaluates them against each candidate answer, with scoring unchanged (Eq.~\ref{eq:score}). If the oracle produces large gains while self-extraction does not, the failure localizes to extraction quality. Results appear in \S\ref{sec:oracle}.
93	
94	\subsection{Process-Level Independence}
95	\label{sec:process-kappa-method}
96	
97	Answer-level Fleiss' $\kappa$ may underestimate the true independence bottleneck. Two traces that follow nearly identical reasoning can reach different final answers; $\kappa$ treats such pairs as disagreeing, overestimating independence. We quantify process-level similarity via pairwise cosine similarity over TF-IDF representations of chain-of-thought traces. For problem $q$ with $K$ traces:
98	\begin{equation}
99	\label{eq:process-sim}
100	\bar{s}(q) = \frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \sum_{i < j} \cos(\mathbf{v}_i, \mathbf{v}_j),
101	\end{equation}
102	where $\mathbf{v}_i$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$. When $\bar{s} \gg \kappa$, reasoning processes are more homogeneous than answers suggest. This metric applies to any trace aggregation method, not only SICA.
103	
104	\subsection{Scope}
105	\label{sec:scope}
106	
107	SICA uses \emph{same-model self-extraction}: the same LLM generates traces and extracts constraints. We test more than twenty remediation strategies: nine prompt-level spanning six dimensions, three method-level (ICG, Contrastive, Debate), and ten additional directions targeting the independence bottleneck from different angles (\S\ref{sec:remediation}). Methods using trained extractors, external provers, or multi-model ensembles fall outside scope.
```

</details>

    "oldString": "\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa-method}",
    "newString": "\\subsection{Cross-Architecture NLI Verification}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch-method}\n\nThe oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) implies that external signal---not same-model refinement---is needed.\nAs a proof-of-concept, we test whether a lightweight cross-architecture model can supply such signal.\nDeBERTa-v3-small \\citep{he2021deberta}, fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}, classifies each (premise, conclusion) pair as \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}.\nIn \\emph{combo} mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC votes via a weight parameter $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times, combined with the $K$ SC votes, and the majority is selected.\nThis 44M-parameter model shares no architecture, training data, or reasoning process with the 7B reasoning LLM, providing a genuinely independent signal.\nResults appear in \\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}.\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa-method}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Self-Consistency and the Condorcet Independence Assumption}\n\\label{sec:condorcet}\n\nSelf-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc} samples $K$ reasoning traces and selects the majority answer. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees that if $K$ independent voters are each correct with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth as $K \\to \\infty$ \\citep{austensmith1996}. LLM traces violate this independence: all $K$ samples come from the same model with only temperature as variation. Following \\citet{ladha1992correlated} and the design-effect framework of \\citet{kish1965survey}, we define effective sample size using Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa} as proxy for intraclass correlation:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:effk}\nK_{\\text{eff}} = \\frac{K}{1 + (K - 1) \\cdot \\kappa}.\n\\end{equation}\nIn our experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82 across instruction-tuned pairs (0.91 for thinking mode), yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} \\approx 1.2\\text{--}3.6$ for $K = 12$. This \\emph{independence bottleneck} motivates our investigation.\n\n\\subsection{SICA Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:pipeline}\n\nSymbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) extends SC by extracting formal constraints from traces and selecting the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\n\n\\paragraph{Trace generation.} Given question $q$, an LLM generates $K$ traces $\\{t_1, \\ldots, t_K\\}$ at temperature $T = 0.7$. Each trace $t_i$ yields answer $a_i$.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint extraction.}\\label{sec:constraint-extraction} The same LLM extracts FOL constraints from each trace at $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$. The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompts}). The \\emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence; each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces. The objective finds the largest-weight satisfiable subset:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:maxsat}\n\\mathcal{C}^* = \\arg\\max_{\\mathcal{S} \\subseteq \\mathcal{C}} \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{S}} w_c \\quad \\text{s.t.} \\quad \\mathcal{S} \\text{ is satisfiable.}\n\\end{equation}\nZ3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} solves this with a 10-second timeout. Answer assertion constraints ($\\texttt{answer\\_a} == \\texttt{True}$, weight 1) ensure every candidate receives a nonzero base score, making SICA a strict generalization of SC.\n\n\\paragraph{Scoring.} Let $\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus} = \\mathcal{C} \\setminus \\mathcal{C}^*$. Each candidate $a$ is scored by:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:score}\n\\text{score}(a) = \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^*} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a) - \\alpha \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if $c$ was extracted from any trace producing answer $a$, and $\\alpha = 0.5$. The selected answer is $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a} \\text{score}(a)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Degeneration condition.} If self-extracted constraints are coupled to their source answer, MAX-SAT retains nearly all ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| \\approx 0$), and scoring degenerates to:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:degenerate}\n\\text{score}(a) \\approx \\sum_{\\substack{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^* \\\\ \\text{source}(c) \\to a}} w_c,\n\\end{equation}\nequivalent to majority voting. We show in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias} that this occurs in practice: ${\\sim}$92\\% of constraints are discriminative but mirror the model's answer distribution (Appendix~\\ref{app:extraction},~\\ref{app:ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0$ (fraction of questions with $>1$ distinct answer) is a zero-cost diagnostic computable before extraction. When $C_0 < 5\\%$, SICA produces the same output as SC.\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:c0-def}\nC_0 = \\frac{|\\{q \\in \\mathcal{Q} : |\\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\\}|}{|\\mathcal{Q}|}.\n\\end{equation}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n    node distance=0.4cm and 0.35cm,\n    box/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, minimum height=0.6cm,\n                text width=1.6cm, align=center, font=\\scriptsize},\n    arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick},\n    darr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick, densely dashed},\n    lbl/.style={font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!70!black},\n    ibottle/.style={draw=red!70!black, densely dashed, rounded corners=3pt,\n                    inner sep=3pt},\n]\n\\node[box] (q) {Question $q$};\n\\node[box, right=of q] (sample) {Sample $K$\\\\traces};\n\\node[box, right=of sample] (extract) {Extract FOL\\\\constraints};\n\\node[box, right=of extract] (dedup) {Dedup.\\ +\\\\weight};\n\\node[box, right=of dedup] (maxsat) {MAX-SAT\\\\(Z3)};\n\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of maxsat, fill=gray!12] (ans) {Answer $a^*$};\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of sample, fill=gray!12] (sc) {Majority\\\\vote};\n\n\\draw[arr] (q) -- (sample);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- (extract);\n\\draw[arr] (extract) -- (dedup);\n\\draw[arr] (dedup) -- (maxsat);\n\\draw[arr] (maxsat) -- (ans);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- node[lbl, right, yshift=0pt] {SC} (sc);\n\n\\node[lbl, above=0.01cm of maxsat] {SICA};\n\n\\node[ibottle, fit=(sample)(extract), label={[font=\\scriptsize\\itshape, text=red!70!black]above:Independence Bottleneck}] {};\n\n\\draw[darr, gray!60] ([xshift=-0.1cm]dedup.south) -- ++(0,-0.35) node[lbl, below, yshift=-0.05cm] {oracle: gold FOL};\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n\\caption{The SICA pipeline. SC selects the majority answer from $K$ traces. SICA extracts FOL constraints, deduplicates, and solves MAX-SAT via Z3. Red dashed box: the independence bottleneck. Dashed arrow: oracle variant with gold FOL.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Oracle Control Design}\n\\label{sec:oracle-design}\n\nTo isolate whether the bottleneck lies in extraction or aggregation, we introduce an oracle control, following the compositionality-gap methodology of \\citet{press2023compositionality}. Gold FOL annotations (FOLIO) or DSL rules (ProofWriter) replace self-extracted constraints; Z3 evaluates them against each candidate answer, with scoring unchanged (Eq.~\\ref{eq:score}). If the oracle produces large gains while self-extraction does not, the failure localizes to extraction quality. Results appear in \\S\\ref{sec:oracle}.\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa-method}\n\nAnswer-level Fleiss' $\\kappa$ may underestimate the true independence bottleneck. Two traces that follow nearly identical reasoning can reach different final answers; $\\kappa$ treats such pairs as disagreeing, overestimating independence. We quantify process-level similarity via pairwise cosine similarity over TF-IDF representations of chain-of-thought traces. For problem $q$ with $K$ traces:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\bar{s}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i, \\mathbf{v}_j),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$. When $\\bar{s} \\gg \\kappa$, reasoning processes are more homogeneous than answers suggest. This metric applies to any trace aggregation method, not only SICA.\n\n\\subsection{Scope}\n\\label{sec:scope}\n\nSICA uses \\emph{same-model self-extraction}: the same LLM generates traces and extracts constraints. We test more than twenty remediation strategies: nine prompt-level spanning six dimensions, three method-level (ICG, Contrastive, Debate), and ten additional directions targeting the independence bottleneck from different angles (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}). Methods using trained extractors, external provers, or multi-model ensembles fall outside scope.",
